========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, I try. I’ll literally tell myself, “Okay, stop, just focus on this one thing,” or “You already checked that,” but it doesn’t feel like it listens. One worry just immediately turns into the next one.

Like I’ll start out thinking, “Did I miss something on my work calendar?” and then I’m checking that, and then while I’m checking that I remember a tuition payment or my budget, so I open that too, and then I’ll think about an assignment deadline, and suddenly I’ve got three different tabs or apps open and I’m re-checking the same things over and over. And the whole time I’m telling myself I should just pick one thing and move on, but I can’t seem to actually do that.

It’s frustrating because it feels like I’m trying to be responsible, but I end up spending so much time checking and rechecking that I’m not really getting anything done. And even when I do “finish” checking, it doesn’t stay finished in my head.

It’s not only work and school, although those are definitely the easiest places for it to latch onto. Like, I’ll start with something concrete — an assignment, a meeting, a deadline — but then it spreads out way past that.

My mind jumps around a lot. One minute I’m worrying that I’m falling behind at work or that I missed something important, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a bad investment and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it becomes, “What if I can’t actually handle all of this and my family can tell I’m not doing as well as I should be?” It’s like it turns into this bigger judgment about my whole life, not just the one thing in front of me.

Even small things don’t stay small in my head. If I make a minor mistake or forget to respond to something, it feels like it could somehow snowball and mess up my job, or school, or my future plans. I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real, like everything is connected and one little thing could derail everything.

It’s more like I’m tense and exhausted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to the demands of work and your graduate program, or do they happen even when those aren’t the focus?\n\nPatient: It’s not only work and school, although those are definitely the easiest places for it to latch onto. One minute I’m worrying that I’m falling behind at work or that I missed something important, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a bad investment and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it becomes, “What if I can’t actually handle all of this and my family can tell I’m not doing as well as I should be?” It’s like it turns into this bigger judgment about my whole life, not just the one thing in front of me.\n\nEven small things don’t stay small in my head. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s honestly a huge part of why I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.

I’ll get into bed in my apartment and I’ll have already closed my laptop, like I’m trying to make it clear to myself that the day is over, but my brain just keeps going as if I’m still working. I’ll be lying there in the dark mentally going back through work deadlines, like, “Did I miss something on the calendar? Did I respond to that email? Was there something due tomorrow?” And then it’ll jump to tuition payments or my budget, and then to class assignments, and I’m not even opening the laptop at that point, but it feels like I’m still checking everything in my head.

And it can go on for hours. It’s not like I’m peacefully resting and just not sleeping. I’m actively thinking and rechecking and trying to reassure myself, but then the reassurance doesn’t stick. Even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. Like technically I slept, but it doesn’t feel like my mind ever stopped running.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean, I know work and school are genuinely a lot, so part of me wants to say, “Of course anyone would be stressed.” But when I compare it to other people, it feels like mine goes way beyond normal stress.

Other people seem able to say, “Okay, I have a deadline, I’ll deal with it,” and then move on. For me, my mind keeps jumping all over the place. I’ll start with worrying that I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a mistake and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it turns into, “What if my family realizes I can’t handle things?” It’s like one concern opens up five more.

And small things don’t feel small to me. If I miss an email or I’m not completely on top of something, I start treating it like it could somehow derail my job, or school, or my whole future. I know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, and I don’t think everyone around me is spiraling like that over the same things. So yes, I do think my worry is much stronger than what most people would feel.

I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real, like everything is connected and one little thing could derail everything.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t fall asleep, is it usually because your mind is busy worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s honestly a huge part of why I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.\n\nI’ll get into bed in my apartment and I’ll have already closed my laptop, like I’m trying to make it clear to myself that the day is over, but my brain just keeps going as if I’m still working. I’ll be lying there in the dark mentally going back through work deadlines, like, “Did I miss something on the calendar? Did I respond to that email? Was there something due tomorrow?” And then it’ll jump to tuition payments or my budget, and then to class assignments, and I’m not even opening the laptop at that point, but it feels like I’m still checking everything in my head.\n\nAnd it can go on for hours. It’s not like I’m peacefully resting and just not sleeping. I’m actively thinking and rechecking and trying to reassure myself, but then the reassurance doesn’t stick. Even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. Like technically I slept, but it doesn’t feel like my mind ever stopped running.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is much stronger than what most people would feel about balancing work and school?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know work and school are genuinely a lot, so part of me wants to say, “Of course anyone would be stressed.” But when I compare it to other people, it feels like mine goes way beyond normal stress.\n\nOther people seem able to say, “Okay, I have a deadline, I’ll deal with it,” and then move on. For me, my mind keeps jumping all over the place. I’ll start with worrying that I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a mistake and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it turns into, “What if my family realizes I can’t handle things?” It’s like one concern opens up five more.\n\nAnd small things don’t feel small to me. If I miss an email or I’m not completely on top of something, I start treating it like it could somehow derail my job, or school, or my whole future. I know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, and I don’t think everyone around me is spiraling like that over the same things. I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know I feel keyed up a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it “restless” exactly, or if that’s just me being busy and tense.

It really started when I began this evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst.\n\nAt first I thought it was just, you know, an adjustment period — like anyone would be stressed trying to work all day and then do school at night. But it didn’t really settle down. It’s been pretty constant since then, like the worrying just became part of my normal day-to-day routine.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you feel able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try. I’ll literally tell myself, “Okay, stop, just focus on this one thing,” or “You already checked that,” but it doesn’t feel like it listens. One worry just immediately turns into the next one.\n\nLike I’ll start out thinking, “Did I miss something on my work calendar?” and then I’m checking that, and then while I’m checking that I remember a tuition payment or my budget, so I open that too, and then I’ll think about an assignment deadline, and suddenly I’ve got three different tabs or apps open and I’m re-checking the same things over and over. And the whole time I’m telling myself I should just pick one thing and move on, but I can’t seem to actually do that.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it feels like I’m trying to be responsible, but I end up spending so much time checking and rechecking that I’m not really getting anything done. And even when I do “finish” checking, it doesn’t stay finished in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I feel depressed most days, at least not in the way I think you mean, like constantly sad or hopeless. I do get worn down and frustrated with myself, and sometimes I feel kind of heavy because I’m tired of my brain always doing this, but it feels more like being overwhelmed than depressed.\n\nThere are days where I’m quieter or I don’t feel like dealing with people as much, but I don’t know that I’d call it being down most days. It’s more like I’m tense and exhausted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to the demands of work and your graduate program, or do they happen even when those aren’t the focus?\n\nPatient: It’s not only work and school, although those are definitely the easiest places for it to latch onto. Like, I’ll start with something concrete — an assignment, a meeting, a deadline — but then it spreads out way past that.\n\nMy mind jumps around a lot. One minute I’m worrying that I’m falling behind at work or that I missed something important, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a bad investment and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it becomes, “What if I can’t actually handle all of this and my family can tell I’m not doing as well as I should be?” It’s like it turns into this bigger judgment about my whole life, not just the one thing in front of me.\n\nEven small things don’t stay small in my head. If I make a minor mistake or forget to respond to something, it feels like it could somehow snowball and mess up my job, or school, or my future plans. I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real, like everything is connected and one little thing could derail everything.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t fall asleep, is it usually because your mind is busy worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s honestly a huge part of why I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.\n\nI’ll get into bed in my apartment and I’ll have already closed my laptop, like I’m trying to make it clear to myself that the day is over, but my brain just keeps going as if I’m still working. I’ll be lying there in the dark mentally going back through work deadlines, like, “Did I miss something on the calendar? Did I respond to that email? Was there something due tomorrow?” And then it’ll jump to tuition payments or my budget, and then to class assignments, and I’m not even opening the laptop at that point, but it feels like I’m still checking everything in my head.\n\nAnd it can go on for hours. It’s not like I’m peacefully resting and just not sleeping. I’m actively thinking and rechecking and trying to reassure myself, but then the reassurance doesn’t stick. Even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. Like technically I slept, but it doesn’t feel like my mind ever stopped running.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is much stronger than what most people would feel about balancing work and school?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know work and school are genuinely a lot, so part of me wants to say, “Of course anyone would be stressed.” But when I compare it to other people, it feels like mine goes way beyond normal stress.\n\nOther people seem able to say, “Okay, I have a deadline, I’ll deal with it,” and then move on. For me, my mind keeps jumping all over the place. I’ll start with worrying that I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a mistake and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it turns into, “What if my family realizes I can’t handle things?” It’s like one concern opens up five more.\n\nAnd small things don’t feel small to me. If I miss an email or I’m not completely on top of something, I start treating it like it could somehow derail my job, or school, or my whole future. I know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, and I don’t think everyone around me is spiraling like that over the same things. So yes, I do think my worry is much stronger than what most people would feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know I feel keyed up a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it “restless” exactly, or if that’s just me being busy and tense.\n\nDuring the day I do feel like I’m kind of bracing for something, if that makes sense. Like I’m not always able to fully settle into whatever I’m doing. But I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically restless, like pacing or needing to move around, versus just feeling internally on edge.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. I can say I don’t usually feel calm in my body during the day, but I’m not sure how often it would count as restlessness.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that happens a lot. It’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because it makes everything take so much longer than it should.

Like, I’ll be sitting with a financial model for work, and I’m technically looking at it — the spreadsheet is open, I’m staring at the numbers — but then I realize I haven’t actually processed what I’m looking at. My head has already gone off into, “What if this is wrong? What if I missed something? What if someone catches a mistake and it reflects badly on me?” And then I have to pull myself back and figure out where I even was.

It happens with school too. I’ll be trying to get through a reading, and I’ll reread the same paragraph five times, maybe more, and I still can’t tell you what it said because while my eyes were moving across the page, my mind was somewhere else entirely. It’s like I’m reading, but I’m also running this whole separate panic checklist in the background about deadlines, money, whether I’m falling behind, all of it.

Sometimes it’s not even that my mind goes completely blank, exactly. It’s more like it gets hijacked. I’ll start on one task, and then the worry pulls me away before I can really hold onto the information. And then I get more anxious because I’m losing time, which makes it even harder to concentrate. So yes, definitely — it interferes with my focus pretty badly.

But I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically restless, like pacing or needing to move around, versus just feeling internally on edge.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. I can say I don’t usually feel calm in my body during the day, but I’m not sure how often it would count as restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind goes blank or gets pulled away by worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because it makes everything take so much longer than it should.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting with a financial model for work, and I’m technically looking at it — the spreadsheet is open, I’m staring at the numbers — but then I realize I haven’t actually processed what I’m looking at. My head has already gone off into, “What if this is wrong? What if I missed something? What if someone catches a mistake and it reflects badly on me?” And then I have to pull myself back and figure out where I even was.\n\nIt happens with school too. I’ll be trying to get through a reading, and I’ll reread the same paragraph five times, maybe more, and I still can’t tell you what it said because while my eyes were moving across the page, my mind was somewhere else entirely. It’s like I’m reading, but I’m also running this whole separate panic checklist in the background about deadlines, money, whether I’m falling behind, all of it.\n\nSometimes it’s not even that my mind goes completely blank, exactly. It’s more like it gets hijacked. I’ll start on one task, and then the worry pulls me away before I can really hold onto the information. I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I feel tense a lot in a general way, but I don’t know if I’ve specifically noticed my muscles being tight, if that makes sense.

Sleep is kind of where it shows up the most because there’s nothing else to distract me, but during the day it’s like my mind keeps hopping from one thing to the next.\n\nI’ll be worrying that I’m falling behind at work, even if no one has said I am, and then suddenly I’m thinking about school and whether I’m wasting money on it or if I made the wrong choice, and then it turns into, like, what if my family thinks I can’t handle my life or I’m not doing enough. It’s like every small thing feels bigger than it should. If I miss one email or I’m unsure about one assignment or one bill, my brain treats it like it could somehow mess up everything I’ve worked for or derail my whole future. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been worrying like this most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I’d say it’s been about a year and three months now. It really started when I began this evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst.\n\nAt first I thought it was just, you know, an adjustment period — like anyone would be stressed trying to work all day and then do school at night. But it didn’t really settle down. It’s been pretty constant since then, like the worrying just became part of my normal day-to-day routine.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you feel able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try. I’ll literally tell myself, “Okay, stop, just focus on this one thing,” or “You already checked that,” but it doesn’t feel like it listens. One worry just immediately turns into the next one.\n\nLike I’ll start out thinking, “Did I miss something on my work calendar?” and then I’m checking that, and then while I’m checking that I remember a tuition payment or my budget, so I open that too, and then I’ll think about an assignment deadline, and suddenly I’ve got three different tabs or apps open and I’m re-checking the same things over and over. And the whole time I’m telling myself I should just pick one thing and move on, but I can’t seem to actually do that.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it feels like I’m trying to be responsible, but I end up spending so much time checking and rechecking that I’m not really getting anything done. And even when I do “finish” checking, it doesn’t stay finished in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I feel depressed most days, at least not in the way I think you mean, like constantly sad or hopeless. I do get worn down and frustrated with myself, and sometimes I feel kind of heavy because I’m tired of my brain always doing this, but it feels more like being overwhelmed than depressed.\n\nThere are days where I’m quieter or I don’t feel like dealing with people as much, but I don’t know that I’d call it being down most days. It’s more like I’m tense and exhausted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to the demands of work and your graduate program, or do they happen even when those aren’t the focus?\n\nPatient: It’s not only work and school, although those are definitely the easiest places for it to latch onto. Like, I’ll start with something concrete — an assignment, a meeting, a deadline — but then it spreads out way past that.\n\nMy mind jumps around a lot. One minute I’m worrying that I’m falling behind at work or that I missed something important, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a bad investment and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it becomes, “What if I can’t actually handle all of this and my family can tell I’m not doing as well as I should be?” It’s like it turns into this bigger judgment about my whole life, not just the one thing in front of me.\n\nEven small things don’t stay small in my head. If I make a minor mistake or forget to respond to something, it feels like it could somehow snowball and mess up my job, or school, or my future plans. I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real, like everything is connected and one little thing could derail everything.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t fall asleep, is it usually because your mind is busy worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s honestly a huge part of why I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.\n\nI’ll get into bed in my apartment and I’ll have already closed my laptop, like I’m trying to make it clear to myself that the day is over, but my brain just keeps going as if I’m still working. I’ll be lying there in the dark mentally going back through work deadlines, like, “Did I miss something on the calendar? Did I respond to that email? Was there something due tomorrow?” And then it’ll jump to tuition payments or my budget, and then to class assignments, and I’m not even opening the laptop at that point, but it feels like I’m still checking everything in my head.\n\nAnd it can go on for hours. It’s not like I’m peacefully resting and just not sleeping. I’m actively thinking and rechecking and trying to reassure myself, but then the reassurance doesn’t stick. Even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. Like technically I slept, but it doesn’t feel like my mind ever stopped running.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is much stronger than what most people would feel about balancing work and school?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know work and school are genuinely a lot, so part of me wants to say, “Of course anyone would be stressed.” But when I compare it to other people, it feels like mine goes way beyond normal stress.\n\nOther people seem able to say, “Okay, I have a deadline, I’ll deal with it,” and then move on. For me, my mind keeps jumping all over the place. I’ll start with worrying that I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a mistake and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it turns into, “What if my family realizes I can’t handle things?” It’s like one concern opens up five more.\n\nAnd small things don’t feel small to me. If I miss an email or I’m not completely on top of something, I start treating it like it could somehow derail my job, or school, or my whole future. I know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, and I don’t think everyone around me is spiraling like that over the same things. So yes, I do think my worry is much stronger than what most people would feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know I feel keyed up a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it “restless” exactly, or if that’s just me being busy and tense.\n\nDuring the day I do feel like I’m kind of bracing for something, if that makes sense. Like I’m not always able to fully settle into whatever I’m doing. But I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically restless, like pacing or needing to move around, versus just feeling internally on edge.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. I can say I don’t usually feel calm in my body during the day, but I’m not sure how often it would count as restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind goes blank or gets pulled away by worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because it makes everything take so much longer than it should.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting with a financial model for work, and I’m technically looking at it — the spreadsheet is open, I’m staring at the numbers — but then I realize I haven’t actually processed what I’m looking at. My head has already gone off into, “What if this is wrong? What if I missed something? What if someone catches a mistake and it reflects badly on me?” And then I have to pull myself back and figure out where I even was.\n\nIt happens with school too. I’ll be trying to get through a reading, and I’ll reread the same paragraph five times, maybe more, and I still can’t tell you what it said because while my eyes were moving across the page, my mind was somewhere else entirely. It’s like I’m reading, but I’m also running this whole separate panic checklist in the background about deadlines, money, whether I’m falling behind, all of it.\n\nSometimes it’s not even that my mind goes completely blank, exactly. It’s more like it gets hijacked. I’ll start on one task, and then the worry pulls me away before I can really hold onto the information. And then I get more anxious because I’m losing time, which makes it even harder to concentrate. So yes, definitely — it interferes with my focus pretty badly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I feel tense a lot in a general way, but I don’t know if I’ve specifically noticed my muscles being tight, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I don’t usually stop and think, “Oh, my shoulders are clenched,” or “my jaw is tight.” I’m more aware of feeling wound up overall. Sometimes by the end of the day I feel sort of physically tired or like I’ve been holding myself rigid, but I couldn’t confidently say how often that’s actual muscle tension versus just being stressed and sitting at a desk for hours.\n\nSo… maybe, but I haven’t paid close enough attention to answer that clearly. If you mean do I feel relaxed in my body most of the time, probably not, but I don’t have a specific muscle-tension thing I can point to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Like I’m not always able to fully settle into whatever I’m doing. But I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically restless, like pacing or needing to move around, versus just feeling internally on edge.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. I can say I don’t usually feel calm in my body during the day, but I’m not sure how often it would count as restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind goes blank or gets pulled away by worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because it makes everything take so much longer than it should.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting with a financial model for work, and I’m technically looking at it — the spreadsheet is open, I’m staring at the numbers — but then I realize I haven’t actually processed what I’m looking at. My head has already gone off into, “What if this is wrong? What if I missed something? What if someone catches a mistake and it reflects badly on me?” And then I have to pull myself back and figure out where I even was.\n\nIt happens with school too. I’ll be trying to get through a reading, and I’ll reread the same paragraph five times, maybe more, and I still can’t tell you what it said because while my eyes were moving across the page, my mind was somewhere else entirely. It’s like I’m reading, but I’m also running this whole separate panic checklist in the background about deadlines, money, whether I’m falling behind, all of it.\n\nSometimes it’s not even that my mind goes completely blank, exactly. It’s more like it gets hijacked. I’ll start on one task, and then the worry pulls me away before I can really hold onto the information. And then I get more anxious because I’m losing time, which makes it even harder to concentrate. So yes, definitely — it interferes with my focus pretty badly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I feel tense a lot in a general way, but I don’t know if I’ve specifically noticed my muscles being tight, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I don’t usually stop and think, “Oh, my shoulders are clenched,” or “my jaw is tight.” I’m more aware of feeling wound up overall. Sometimes by the end of the day I feel sort of physically tired or like I’ve been holding myself rigid, but I couldn’t confidently say how often that’s actual muscle tension versus just being stressed and sitting at a desk for hours.\n\nSo… maybe, but I haven’t paid close enough attention to answer that clearly. If you mean do I feel relaxed in my body most of the time, probably not, but I don’t have a specific muscle-tension thing I can point to.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf your work and school demands suddenly eased up, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / management_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"My mind keeps jumping from whether I’m falling behind at work, to whether I’m wasting money on school, to whether my family will think I can’t handle things, and I treat every small issue like it could derail my whole future.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think it would mostly go away. I mean, I want to say it would help, because obviously work and school are huge triggers, and if those eased up I’d probably have fewer immediate things to panic about. But I don’t think the worry is only about the workload.

It’s like my mind just… shifts targets. If I’m worried about work, it’s, “Am I falling behind? Did I miss something? Am I going to mess up and ruin my credibility?” And then if I try to tell myself, okay, work is manageable, then it jumps to school — like, “Am I wasting money on this? What if I’m investing all this time and it doesn’t pay off? What if I made the wrong choice?” And then somehow it becomes about my family, like, “Are they going to think I can’t handle my life? Am I disappointing people?”

So even if the actual demands got lighter, I think I’d probably still find something to worry about. I tend to take these small issues and treat them like they could derail my whole future. Like one email, one assignment, one expense suddenly feels connected to whether everything is going to fall apart. So… no, I don’t think it would disappear. It might change shape, but I think the worry would still be there.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um… I’m here because my sleep has basically just fallen apart. I’ll get into bed in my apartment and I’ll have already closed my laptop, like I’m technically “done” for the night, but my brain just keeps going. I’ll lie there for hours mentally rechecking work deadlines, whether I paid tuition or what’s due next, class assignments… just going through everything over and over like I’m going to miss something important.\n\nAnd even when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel like I actually rested. I wake up feeling like I never really turned off, like part of me was still working through all of it the whole night. It’s gotten to the point where I dread going to bed because I know I’m probably just going to lie there thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying like this about a lot of different parts of your life, not just sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just sleep. Sleep is kind of where it shows up the most because there’s nothing else to distract me, but during the day it’s like my mind keeps hopping from one thing to the next.\n\nI’ll be worrying that I’m falling behind at work, even if no one has said I am, and then suddenly I’m thinking about school and whether I’m wasting money on it or if I made the wrong choice, and then it turns into, like, what if my family thinks I can’t handle my life or I’m not doing enough. It’s like every small thing feels bigger than it should. If I miss one email or I’m unsure about one assignment or one bill, my brain treats it like it could somehow mess up everything I’ve worked for or derail my whole future. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been worrying like this most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I’d say it’s been about a year and three months now. It really started when I began this evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst.\n\nAt first I thought it was just, you know, an adjustment period — like anyone would be stressed trying to work all day and then do school at night. But it didn’t really settle down. It’s been pretty constant since then, like the worrying just became part of my normal day-to-day routine.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you feel able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try. I’ll literally tell myself, “Okay, stop, just focus on this one thing,” or “You already checked that,” but it doesn’t feel like it listens. One worry just immediately turns into the next one.\n\nLike I’ll start out thinking, “Did I miss something on my work calendar?” and then I’m checking that, and then while I’m checking that I remember a tuition payment or my budget, so I open that too, and then I’ll think about an assignment deadline, and suddenly I’ve got three different tabs or apps open and I’m re-checking the same things over and over. And the whole time I’m telling myself I should just pick one thing and move on, but I can’t seem to actually do that.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it feels like I’m trying to be responsible, but I end up spending so much time checking and rechecking that I’m not really getting anything done. And even when I do “finish” checking, it doesn’t stay finished in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I feel depressed most days, at least not in the way I think you mean, like constantly sad or hopeless. I do get worn down and frustrated with myself, and sometimes I feel kind of heavy because I’m tired of my brain always doing this, but it feels more like being overwhelmed than depressed.\n\nThere are days where I’m quieter or I don’t feel like dealing with people as much, but I don’t know that I’d call it being down most days. It’s more like I’m tense and exhausted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to the demands of work and your graduate program, or do they happen even when those aren’t the focus?\n\nPatient: It’s not only work and school, although those are definitely the easiest places for it to latch onto. Like, I’ll start with something concrete — an assignment, a meeting, a deadline — but then it spreads out way past that.\n\nMy mind jumps around a lot. One minute I’m worrying that I’m falling behind at work or that I missed something important, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a bad investment and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it becomes, “What if I can’t actually handle all of this and my family can tell I’m not doing as well as I should be?” It’s like it turns into this bigger judgment about my whole life, not just the one thing in front of me.\n\nEven small things don’t stay small in my head. If I make a minor mistake or forget to respond to something, it feels like it could somehow snowball and mess up my job, or school, or my future plans. I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real, like everything is connected and one little thing could derail everything.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t fall asleep, is it usually because your mind is busy worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s honestly a huge part of why I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.\n\nI’ll get into bed in my apartment and I’ll have already closed my laptop, like I’m trying to make it clear to myself that the day is over, but my brain just keeps going as if I’m still working. I’ll be lying there in the dark mentally going back through work deadlines, like, “Did I miss something on the calendar? Did I respond to that email? Was there something due tomorrow?” And then it’ll jump to tuition payments or my budget, and then to class assignments, and I’m not even opening the laptop at that point, but it feels like I’m still checking everything in my head.\n\nAnd it can go on for hours. It’s not like I’m peacefully resting and just not sleeping. I’m actively thinking and rechecking and trying to reassure myself, but then the reassurance doesn’t stick. Even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. Like technically I slept, but it doesn’t feel like my mind ever stopped running.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is much stronger than what most people would feel about balancing work and school?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know work and school are genuinely a lot, so part of me wants to say, “Of course anyone would be stressed.” But when I compare it to other people, it feels like mine goes way beyond normal stress.\n\nOther people seem able to say, “Okay, I have a deadline, I’ll deal with it,” and then move on. For me, my mind keeps jumping all over the place. I’ll start with worrying that I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a mistake and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it turns into, “What if my family realizes I can’t handle things?” It’s like one concern opens up five more.\n\nAnd small things don’t feel small to me. If I miss an email or I’m not completely on top of something, I start treating it like it could somehow derail my job, or school, or my whole future. I know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, and I don’t think everyone around me is spiraling like that over the same things. So yes, I do think my worry is much stronger than what most people would feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know I feel keyed up a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it “restless” exactly, or if that’s just me being busy and tense.\n\nDuring the day I do feel like I’m kind of bracing for something, if that makes sense. Like I’m not always able to fully settle into whatever I’m doing. But I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically restless, like pacing or needing to move around, versus just feeling internally on edge.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. I can say I don’t usually feel calm in my body during the day, but I’m not sure how often it would count as restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind goes blank or gets pulled away by worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because it makes everything take so much longer than it should.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting with a financial model for work, and I’m technically looking at it — the spreadsheet is open, I’m staring at the numbers — but then I realize I haven’t actually processed what I’m looking at. My head has already gone off into, “What if this is wrong? What if I missed something? What if someone catches a mistake and it reflects badly on me?” And then I have to pull myself back and figure out where I even was.\n\nIt happens with school too. It’s more like it gets hijacked. I’ll start on one task, and then the worry pulls me away before I can really hold onto the information. And then I get more anxious because I’m losing time, which makes it even harder to concentrate. So yes, definitely — it interferes with my focus pretty badly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, I think the sleep problem is almost completely tied to the worrying. Like, if my brain actually shut off when I got into bed, I don’t think I’d have the same trouble falling asleep.

What happens now is I’ll be in my apartment, laptop closed, lights off or mostly off, and I’m technically done for the day — but my mind is still going. I’ll start mentally rechecking work deadlines, like, “Did I respond to that email? Is that model due sooner than I think? Did I forget something important?” And then it shifts to tuition payments or class assignments, or whether I’m falling behind, and suddenly I’ve been lying there for hours.

It’s not like I’m staying up doing something fun or even being productive. I’m just in bed, exhausted, but still reviewing everything over and over. And even when I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never fully turned off, like I didn’t actually get real rest.

So… outside of the worrying, maybe I’d still have the occasional bad night, but I don’t think it would be like this. The worry is what keeps me awake.

And even when I do “finish” checking, it doesn’t stay finished in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I feel depressed most days, at least not in the way I think you mean, like constantly sad or hopeless. I do get worn down and frustrated with myself, and sometimes I feel kind of heavy because I’m tired of my brain always doing this, but it feels more like being overwhelmed than depressed.\n\nThere are days where I’m quieter or I don’t feel like dealing with people as much, but I don’t know that I’d call it being down most days. It’s more like I’m tense and exhausted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to the demands of work and your graduate program, or do they happen even when those aren’t the focus?\n\nPatient: It’s not only work and school, although those are definitely the easiest places for it to latch onto. Like, I’ll start with something concrete — an assignment, a meeting, a deadline — but then it spreads out way past that.\n\nMy mind jumps around a lot. One minute I’m worrying that I’m falling behind at work or that I missed something important, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a bad investment and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it becomes, “What if I can’t actually handle all of this and my family can tell I’m not doing as well as I should be?” It’s like it turns into this bigger judgment about my whole life, not just the one thing in front of me.\n\nEven small things don’t stay small in my head. If I make a minor mistake or forget to respond to something, it feels like it could somehow snowball and mess up my job, or school, or my future plans. I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real, like everything is connected and one little thing could derail everything.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t fall asleep, is it usually because your mind is busy worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s honestly a huge part of why I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.\n\nI’ll get into bed in my apartment and I’ll have already closed my laptop, like I’m trying to make it clear to myself that the day is over, but my brain just keeps going as if I’m still working. I’ll be lying there in the dark mentally going back through work deadlines, like, “Did I miss something on the calendar? Did I respond to that email? Was there something due tomorrow?” And then it’ll jump to tuition payments or my budget, and then to class assignments, and I’m not even opening the laptop at that point, but it feels like I’m still checking everything in my head.\n\nAnd it can go on for hours. It’s not like I’m peacefully resting and just not sleeping. I’m actively thinking and rechecking and trying to reassure myself, but then the reassurance doesn’t stick. Even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. Like technically I slept, but it doesn’t feel like my mind ever stopped running.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is much stronger than what most people would feel about balancing work and school?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know work and school are genuinely a lot, so part of me wants to say, “Of course anyone would be stressed.” But when I compare it to other people, it feels like mine goes way beyond normal stress.\n\nOther people seem able to say, “Okay, I have a deadline, I’ll deal with it,” and then move on. For me, my mind keeps jumping all over the place. I’ll start with worrying that I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a mistake and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it turns into, “What if my family realizes I can’t handle things?” It’s like one concern opens up five more.\n\nAnd small things don’t feel small to me. If I miss an email or I’m not completely on top of something, I start treating it like it could somehow derail my job, or school, or my whole future. I know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, and I don’t think everyone around me is spiraling like that over the same things. So yes, I do think my worry is much stronger than what most people would feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know I feel keyed up a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it “restless” exactly, or if that’s just me being busy and tense.\n\nDuring the day I do feel like I’m kind of bracing for something, if that makes sense. Like I’m not always able to fully settle into whatever I’m doing. But I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically restless, like pacing or needing to move around, versus just feeling internally on edge.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. I can say I don’t usually feel calm in my body during the day, but I’m not sure how often it would count as restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind goes blank or gets pulled away by worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because it makes everything take so much longer than it should.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting with a financial model for work, and I’m technically looking at it — the spreadsheet is open, I’m staring at the numbers — but then I realize I haven’t actually processed what I’m looking at. My head has already gone off into, “What if this is wrong? What if I missed something? What if someone catches a mistake and it reflects badly on me?” And then I have to pull myself back and figure out where I even was.\n\nIt happens with school too. I’ll be trying to get through a reading, and I’ll reread the same paragraph five times, maybe more, and I still can’t tell you what it said because while my eyes were moving across the page, my mind was somewhere else entirely. It’s more like it gets hijacked. I’ll start on one task, and then the worry pulls me away before I can really hold onto the information. Sometimes by the end of the day I feel sort of physically tired or like I’ve been holding myself rigid, but I couldn’t confidently say how often that’s actual muscle tension versus just being stressed and sitting at a desk for hours.\n\nSo… maybe, but I haven’t paid close enough attention to answer that clearly. No, I don’t think so. I mean, if my mind really isn’t caught up in worry, I can usually fall asleep. The problem is that most nights it feels like I can’t get to that place.

I’ll be in my apartment with my laptop closed, like the day is technically over, but I’m still lying there going back through everything in my head — work deadlines, whether I missed something, tuition payments, class assignments, all of it. It’s like my body is in bed but my brain is still at my desk trying to solve things.

So when I’m not doing that mental rechecking, I don’t think sleep itself is the issue. It’s more that I almost never feel fully switched off lately. And even when I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off or rested properly.
=====================================

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F51.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um… I’m here because my sleep has basically just fallen apart. I’ll get into bed in my apartment and I’ll have already closed my laptop, like I’m technically “done” for the night, but my brain just keeps going. I’ll lie there for hours mentally rechecking work deadlines, whether I paid tuition or what’s due next, class assignments… just going through everything over and over like I’m going to miss something important.\n\nAnd even when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel like I actually rested. I wake up feeling like I never really turned off, like part of me was still working through all of it the whole night. It’s gotten to the point where I dread going to bed because I know I’m probably just going to lie there thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying like this about a lot of different parts of your life, not just sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just sleep. Sleep is kind of where it shows up the most because there’s nothing else to distract me, but during the day it’s like my mind keeps hopping from one thing to the next.\n\nI’ll be worrying that I’m falling behind at work, even if no one has said I am, and then suddenly I’m thinking about school and whether I’m wasting money on it or if I made the wrong choice, and then it turns into, like, what if my family thinks I can’t handle my life or I’m not doing enough. It’s like every small thing feels bigger than it should. If I miss one email or I’m unsure about one assignment or one bill, my brain treats it like it could somehow mess up everything I’ve worked for or derail my whole future. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been worrying like this most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I’d say it’s been about a year and three months now. It really started when I began this evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst.\n\nAt first I thought it was just, you know, an adjustment period — like anyone would be stressed trying to work all day and then do school at night. But it didn’t really settle down. It’s been pretty constant since then, like the worrying just became part of my normal day-to-day routine.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you feel able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try. I’ll literally tell myself, “Okay, stop, just focus on this one thing,” or “You already checked that,” but it doesn’t feel like it listens. One worry just immediately turns into the next one.\n\nLike I’ll start out thinking, “Did I miss something on my work calendar?” and then I’m checking that, and then while I’m checking that I remember a tuition payment or my budget, so I open that too, and then I’ll think about an assignment deadline, and suddenly I’ve got three different tabs or apps open and I’m re-checking the same things over and over. And the whole time I’m telling myself I should just pick one thing and move on, but I can’t seem to actually do that.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it feels like I’m trying to be responsible, but I end up spending so much time checking and rechecking that I’m not really getting anything done. And even when I do “finish” checking, it doesn’t stay finished in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I feel depressed most days, at least not in the way I think you mean, like constantly sad or hopeless. I do get worn down and frustrated with myself, and sometimes I feel kind of heavy because I’m tired of my brain always doing this, but it feels more like being overwhelmed than depressed.\n\nThere are days where I’m quieter or I don’t feel like dealing with people as much, but I don’t know that I’d call it being down most days. It’s more like I’m tense and exhausted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to the demands of work and your graduate program, or do they happen even when those aren’t the focus?\n\nPatient: It’s not only work and school, although those are definitely the easiest places for it to latch onto. Like, I’ll start with something concrete — an assignment, a meeting, a deadline — but then it spreads out way past that.\n\nMy mind jumps around a lot. One minute I’m worrying that I’m falling behind at work or that I missed something important, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a bad investment and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it becomes, “What if I can’t actually handle all of this and my family can tell I’m not doing as well as I should be?” It’s like it turns into this bigger judgment about my whole life, not just the one thing in front of me.\n\nEven small things don’t stay small in my head. If I make a minor mistake or forget to respond to something, it feels like it could somehow snowball and mess up my job, or school, or my future plans. I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real, like everything is connected and one little thing could derail everything.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t fall asleep, is it usually because your mind is busy worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s honestly a huge part of why I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.\n\nI’ll get into bed in my apartment and I’ll have already closed my laptop, like I’m trying to make it clear to myself that the day is over, but my brain just keeps going as if I’m still working. I’ll be lying there in the dark mentally going back through work deadlines, like, “Did I miss something on the calendar? Did I respond to that email? Was there something due tomorrow?” And then it’ll jump to tuition payments or my budget, and then to class assignments, and I’m not even opening the laptop at that point, but it feels like I’m still checking everything in my head.\n\nAnd it can go on for hours. It’s not like I’m peacefully resting and just not sleeping. I’m actively thinking and rechecking and trying to reassure myself, but then the reassurance doesn’t stick. Even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. Like technically I slept, but it doesn’t feel like my mind ever stopped running.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is much stronger than what most people would feel about balancing work and school?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know work and school are genuinely a lot, so part of me wants to say, “Of course anyone would be stressed.” But when I compare it to other people, it feels like mine goes way beyond normal stress.\n\nOther people seem able to say, “Okay, I have a deadline, I’ll deal with it,” and then move on. For me, my mind keeps jumping all over the place. I’ll start with worrying that I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a mistake and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it turns into, “What if my family realizes I can’t handle things?” It’s like one concern opens up five more.\n\nAnd small things don’t feel small to me. If I miss an email or I’m not completely on top of something, I start treating it like it could somehow derail my job, or school, or my whole future. I know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, and I don’t think everyone around me is spiraling like that over the same things. So yes, I do think my worry is much stronger than what most people would feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know I feel keyed up a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it “restless” exactly, or if that’s just me being busy and tense.\n\nDuring the day I do feel like I’m kind of bracing for something, if that makes sense. Like I’m not always able to fully settle into whatever I’m doing. But I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m physically restless, like pacing or needing to move around, versus just feeling internally on edge.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. I can say I don’t usually feel calm in my body during the day, but I’m not sure how often it would count as restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind goes blank or gets pulled away by worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because it makes everything take so much longer than it should.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting with a financial model for work, and I’m technically looking at it — the spreadsheet is open, I’m staring at the numbers — but then I realize I haven’t actually processed what I’m looking at. My head has already gone off into, “What if this is wrong? What if I missed something? What if someone catches a mistake and it reflects badly on me?” And then I have to pull myself back and figure out where I even was.\n\nIt happens with school too. I’ll be trying to get through a reading, and I’ll reread the same paragraph five times, maybe more, and I still can’t tell you what it said because while my eyes were moving across the page, my mind was somewhere else entirely. It’s like I’m reading, but I’m also running this whole separate panic checklist in the background about deadlines, money, whether I’m falling behind, all of it.\n\nSometimes it’s not even that my mind goes completely blank, exactly. It’s more like it gets hijacked. I’ll start on one task, and then the worry pulls me away before I can really hold onto the information. And then I get more anxious because I’m losing time, which makes it even harder to concentrate. So yes, definitely — it interferes with my focus pretty badly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I feel tense a lot in a general way, but I don’t know if I’ve specifically noticed my muscles being tight, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I don’t usually stop and think, “Oh, my shoulders are clenched,” or “my jaw is tight.” I’m more aware of feeling wound up overall. Sometimes by the end of the day I feel sort of physically tired or like I’ve been holding myself rigid, but I couldn’t confidently say how often that’s actual muscle tension versus just being stressed and sitting at a desk for hours.\n\nSo… maybe, but I haven’t paid close enough attention to answer that clearly. If you mean do I feel relaxed in my body most of the time, probably not, but I don’t have a specific muscle-tension thing I can point to.\n\nDoctor: If your work and school demands suddenly eased up, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it would mostly go away. I mean, I want to say it would help, because obviously work and school are huge triggers, and if those eased up I’d probably have fewer immediate things to panic about. But I don’t think the worry is only about the workload.\n\nIt’s like my mind just… shifts targets. If I’m worried about work, it’s, “Am I falling behind? Did I miss something? Am I going to mess up and ruin my credibility?” And then if I try to tell myself, okay, work is manageable, then it jumps to school — like, “Am I wasting money on this? What if I’m investing all this time and it doesn’t pay off? What if I made the wrong choice?” And then somehow it becomes about my family, like, “Are they going to think I can’t handle my life? Am I disappointing people?”\n\nSo even if the actual demands got lighter, I think I’d probably still find something to worry about. I tend to take these small issues and treat them like they could derail my whole future. Like one email, one assignment, one expense suddenly feels connected to whether everything is going to fall apart. So… no, I don’t think it would disappear. It might change shape, but I think the worry would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the worrying, do you think you would still have trouble falling asleep?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I think the sleep problem is almost completely tied to the worrying. Like, if my brain actually shut off when I got into bed, I don’t think I’d have the same trouble falling asleep.\n\nWhat happens now is I’ll be in my apartment, laptop closed, lights off or mostly off, and I’m technically done for the day — but my mind is still going. I’ll start mentally rechecking work deadlines, like, “Did I respond to that email? Is that model due sooner than I think? Did I forget something important?” And then it shifts to tuition payments or class assignments, or whether I’m falling behind, and suddenly I’ve been lying there for hours.\n\nIt’s not like I’m staying up doing something fun or even being productive. I’m just in bed, exhausted, but still reviewing everything over and over. And even when I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never fully turned off, like I didn’t actually get real rest.\n\nSo… outside of the worrying, maybe I’d still have the occasional bad night, but I don’t think it would be like this. The worry is what keeps me awake.\n\nDoctor: When your mind is not caught up in worry, do you still have trouble falling asleep?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, if my mind really isn’t caught up in worry, I can usually fall asleep. The problem is that most nights it feels like I can’t get to that place.\n\nI’ll be in my apartment with my laptop closed, like the day is technically over, but I’m still lying there going back through everything in my head — work deadlines, whether I missed something, tuition payments, class assignments, all of it. It’s like my body is in bed but my brain is still at my desk trying to solve things.\n\nSo when I’m not doing that mental rechecking, I don’t think sleep itself is the issue. It’s more that I almost never feel fully switched off lately. And even when I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off or rested properly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’m… not totally sure, honestly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed myself being dramatically more irritable in a way that stands out, like snapping at everyone all the time or anything like that.

I do sometimes feel a little shorter with people, but I’m not sure if that’s actual irritability or just being mentally worn down. Like if someone asks me something when I’m already trying to keep a bunch of things straight in my head, I might feel this quick flash of frustration internally. But I don’t know that I’m acting on it much, or that other people would describe me as more irritable.

So I guess my answer is… maybe a little, but I haven’t really been tracking it, and it’s not the main thing I’ve noticed.

If I make a minor mistake or forget to respond to something, it feels like it could somehow snowball and mess up my job, or school, or my future plans. I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real, like everything is connected and one little thing could derail everything.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t fall asleep, is it usually because your mind is busy worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s honestly a huge part of why I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.\n\nI’ll get into bed in my apartment and I’ll have already closed my laptop, like I’m trying to make it clear to myself that the day is over, but my brain just keeps going as if I’m still working. I’ll be lying there in the dark mentally going back through work deadlines, like, “Did I miss something on the calendar? Did I respond to that email? It’s not like I’m peacefully resting and just not sleeping. I’m actively thinking and rechecking and trying to reassure myself, but then the reassurance doesn’t stick. Even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. I’ll get into bed in my apartment and I’ll have already closed my laptop, like I’m technically “done” for the night, but my brain just keeps going. I’ll lie there for hours mentally rechecking work deadlines, whether I paid tuition or what’s due next, class assignments… just going through everything over and over like I’m going to miss something important.\n\nAnd even when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel like I actually rested. I wake up feeling like I never really turned off, like part of me was still working through all of it the whole night. It’s gotten to the point where I dread going to bed because I know I’m probably just going to lie there thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying like this about a lot of different parts of your life, not just sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just sleep. Sleep is kind of where it shows up the most because there’s nothing else to distract me, but during the day it’s like my mind keeps hopping from one thing to the next.\n\nI’ll be worrying that I’m falling behind at work, even if no one has said I am, and then suddenly I’m thinking about school and whether I’m wasting money on it or if I made the wrong choice, and then it turns into, like, what if my family thinks I can’t handle my life or I’m not doing enough. It’s like every small thing feels bigger than it should. I’d say it’s been about a year and three months now. It really started when I began this evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst.\n\nAt first I thought it was just, you know, an adjustment period — like anyone would be stressed trying to work all day and then do school at night. And the whole time I’m telling myself I should just pick one thing and move on, but I can’t seem to actually do that.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it feels like I’m trying to be responsible, but I end up spending so much time checking and rechecking that I’m not really getting anything done. One minute I’m worrying that I’m falling behind at work or that I missed something important, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a bad investment and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it becomes, “What if I can’t actually handle all of this and my family can tell I’m not doing as well as I should be?” It’s like it turns into this bigger judgment about my whole life, not just the one thing in front of me.\n\nEven small things don’t stay small in my head. If I make a minor mistake or forget to respond to something, it feels like it could somehow snowball and mess up my job, or school, or my future plans. I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real, like everything is connected and one little thing could derail everything.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t fall asleep, is it usually because your mind is busy worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s honestly a huge part of why I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.\n\nI’ll get into bed in my apartment and I’ll have already closed my laptop, like I’m trying to make it clear to myself that the day is over, but my brain just keeps going as if I’m still working. I’ll be lying there in the dark mentally going back through work deadlines, like, “Did I miss something on the calendar? Did I respond to that email? Was there something due tomorrow?” And then it’ll jump to tuition payments or my budget, and then to class assignments, and I’m not even opening the laptop at that point, but it feels like I’m still checking everything in my head.\n\nAnd it can go on for hours. It’s not like I’m peacefully resting and just not sleeping. I’m actively thinking and rechecking and trying to reassure myself, but then the reassurance doesn’t stick. Even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. Like technically I slept, but it doesn’t feel like my mind ever stopped running.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is much stronger than what most people would feel about balancing work and school?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I know work and school are genuinely a lot, so part of me wants to say, “Of course anyone would be stressed.” But when I compare it to other people, it feels like mine goes way beyond normal stress.\n\nOther people seem able to say, “Okay, I have a deadline, I’ll deal with it,” and then move on. For me, my mind keeps jumping all over the place. I’ll start with worrying that I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if school is a mistake and I’m wasting all this money?” And then it turns into, “What if my family realizes I can’t handle things?” It’s like one concern opens up five more.\n\nAnd small things don’t feel small to me. If I miss an email or I’m not completely on top of something, I start treating it like it could somehow derail my job, or school, or my whole future. My head has already gone off into, “What if this is wrong? What if I missed something? I’ll be trying to get through a reading, and I’ll reread the same paragraph five times, maybe more, and I still can’t tell you what it said because while my eyes were moving across the page, my mind was somewhere else entirely. What if I’m investing all this time and it doesn’t pay off? What if I made the wrong choice?” And then somehow it becomes about my family, like, “Are they going to think I can’t handle my life? I tend to take these small issues and treat them like they could derail my whole future. Like one email, one assignment, one expense suddenly feels connected to whether everything is going to fall apart. So… no, I don’t think it would disappear. It might change shape, but I think the worry would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the worrying, do you think you would still have trouble falling asleep?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I think the sleep problem is almost completely tied to the worrying. Like, if my brain actually shut off when I got into bed, I don’t think I’d have the same trouble falling asleep.\n\nWhat happens now is I’ll be in my apartment, laptop closed, lights off or mostly off, and I’m technically done for the day — but my mind is still going. I’ll start mentally rechecking work deadlines, like, “Did I respond to that email? Is that model due sooner than I think? Did I forget something important?” And then it shifts to tuition payments or class assignments, or whether I’m falling behind, and suddenly I’ve been lying there for hours.\n\nIt’s not like I’m staying up doing something fun or even being productive. I’m just in bed, exhausted, but still reviewing everything over and over. And even when I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never fully turned off, like I didn’t actually get real rest.\n\nSo… outside of the worrying, maybe I’d still have the occasional bad night, but I don’t think it would be like this. The worry is what keeps me awake.\n\nDoctor: When your mind is not caught up in worry, do you still have trouble falling asleep?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, if my mind really isn’t caught up in worry, I can usually fall asleep. The problem is that most nights it feels like I can’t get to that place.\n\nI’ll be in my apartment with my laptop closed, like the day is technically over, but I’m still lying there going back through everything in my head — work deadlines, whether I missed something, tuition payments, class assignments, all of it. It’s like my body is in bed but my brain is still at my desk trying to solve things.\n\nSo when I’m not doing that mental rechecking, I don’t think sleep itself is the issue. It’s more that I almost never feel fully switched off lately. And even when I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off or rested properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure, honestly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed myself being dramatically more irritable in a way that stands out, like snapping at everyone all the time or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes feel a little shorter with people, but I’m not sure if that’s actual irritability or just being mentally worn down. Like if someone asks me something when I’m already trying to keep a bunch of things straight in my head, I might feel this quick flash of frustration internally. But I don’t know that I’m acting on it much, or that other people would describe me as more irritable.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe a little, but I haven’t really been tracking it, and it’s not the main thing I’ve noticed."
